Well, I guess I’m here because work has just started to feel… not like my usual work stress. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
A little, yes. I’m still teaching, and I’m not dropping the big things — the kids are getting taught, the day still happens, you know. But I’ve noticed I’m not as on top of the planning pieces.

Like, I’ll sit down to revise a lesson plan and then end up checking three other things, or remembering I need to send something to a parent, and somehow the original thing doesn’t get finished. And then it’s the next day and someone on my team will say, “Did you upload that yet?” and I’ll realize I pushed it back again.

That’s not really like me. I used to be pretty reliable with those deadlines without needing reminders. Now I’m still meeting the basic responsibilities, but some planning deadlines and little task follow-through things have slipped.

I mean, I’ll still talk with other teachers, still go through my routines. Sometimes I don’t feel as… I don’t know, sparked by things maybe, but then something small will happen and I’ll laugh. Like the other day one of the kids was very seriously explaining that our classroom plant “has trust issues,” which — I don’t even know where they get these things.

So I guess I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing less, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.

Yes, a bit. Mostly with the lesson planning side of things. I’m still teaching, and the regular day-to-day with the kids is happening — you sort of have to stay present when twenty-something children all need something at once.

But the planning pieces, the follow-through… those have been harder. I’ll mean to finish something, like upload a plan or revise an activity, and then somehow it gets pushed back. And then someone on my team will remind me, and I’ll think, oh right, I used to just do that without being nudged.

It’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s more than I’m used to.

It’s more that since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been feeling wound up and discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me.\n\nI still get through the school day — I mean, teachers kind of have to, the bell rings whether you’re ready or not — but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without thinking. And then I look at the pile and feel worse about it. So, not hopeless all the time, but definitely more discouraged than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing more worry or anxiety than usual, especially about things related to work or responsibilities outside of school?\n\nPatient: Yes, more than usual, I think. It’s mostly tied to work, though not only during school hours, because I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something and suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer or a lesson plan I meant to revise.\n\nEver since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt wound up in a way that isn’t really like me. I mean, I’ve been through new programs before—there’s always some new initiative with binders and acronyms—but this one has me second-guessing things. I still get through the day, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails I used to handle without even thinking, and then I worry because I know I’m behind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired than usual even after resting or feeling like it takes more effort to get through the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change there. I mean, teaching is tiring anyway — especially with kids this time of year, they get that kind of squirrelly energy after lunch, and you feel like you’ve run a small circus by 3 o’clock.\n\nBut as far as being more tired even after resting… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking my energy like that. I still get through the day. Some evenings I feel worn out, but I sort of chalk that up to the usual school-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or less interested in things for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “Most of the day” and “more days than not” feels very… precise, and I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nI mean, some days I’m definitely discouraged, and I can get kind of stuck in that feeling, but then I’ll be fine talking with a student or dealing with something in the classroom. Kids don’t really let you stay in one mood for too long, you know? One of them will ask if whales have belly buttons or something and suddenly you’re off on that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say yes to that the way you asked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, making decisions, or staying focused on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’m still teaching, and I’m not dropping the big things — the kids are getting taught, the day still happens, you know. But I’ve noticed I’m not as on top of the planning pieces.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to revise a lesson plan and then end up checking three other things, or remembering I need to send something to a parent, and somehow the original thing doesn’t get finished. And then it’s the next day and someone on my team will say, “Did you upload that yet?” and I’ll realize I pushed it back again.\n\nThat’s not really like me. I used to be pretty reliable with those deadlines without needing reminders. Now I’m still meeting the basic responsibilities, but some planning deadlines and little task follow-through things have slipped.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re engaging in your usual activities less often, or that they feel less rewarding than they used to, even if you still enjoy them a little?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… less rewarding is hard for me to say. I still do the things I usually do, at least the main things. School takes up so much space that sometimes I feel like there isn’t much left to compare it to, you know?\n\nI mean, I’ll still talk with other teachers, still go through my routines. Sometimes I don’t feel as… I don’t know, sparked by things maybe, but then something small will happen and I’ll laugh. Like the other day one of the kids was very seriously explaining that our classroom plant “has trust issues,” which — I don’t even know where they get these things.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing less, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, like planning lessons or even following conversations, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Mostly with the lesson planning side of things. I’m still teaching, and the regular day-to-day with the kids is happening — you sort of have to stay present when twenty-something children all need something at once.\n\nBut the planning pieces, the follow-through… those have been harder. I’ll mean to finish something, like upload a plan or revise an activity, and then somehow it gets pushed back. And then someone on my team will remind me, and I’ll think, oh right, I used to just do that without being nudged.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s more than I’m used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Most days, yes… not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, but I do feel more discouraged and kind of wound up than I used to.

It really started after the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. And, you know, I care about that work — I do — but the way it landed was just… a lot. I still get through the school day, the kids are fine, the classroom is running. And then I’ll put off lesson plans or emails that I used to just handle without thinking. So yes, I’d say it’s been there a lot of days, even if I’m still showing up.

So, not hopeless all the time, but definitely more discouraged than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing more worry or anxiety than usual, especially about things related to work or responsibilities outside of school?\n\nPatient: Yes, more than usual, I think. It’s mostly tied to work, though not only during school hours, because I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something and suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer or a lesson plan I meant to revise.\n\nEver since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt wound up in a way that isn’t really like me. I mean, I’ve been through new programs before—there’s always some new initiative with binders and acronyms—but this one has me second-guessing things. I still get through the day, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails I used to handle without even thinking, and then I worry because I know I’m behind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired than usual even after resting or feeling like it takes more effort to get through the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change there. I mean, teaching is tiring anyway — especially with kids this time of year, they get that kind of squirrelly energy after lunch, and you feel like you’ve run a small circus by 3 o’clock.\n\nBut as far as being more tired even after resting… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking my energy like that. I still get through the day. Some evenings I feel worn out, but I sort of chalk that up to the usual school-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or less interested in things for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “Most of the day” and “more days than not” feels very… precise, and I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nI mean, some days I’m definitely discouraged, and I can get kind of stuck in that feeling, but then I’ll be fine talking with a student or dealing with something in the classroom. Kids don’t really let you stay in one mood for too long, you know? One of them will ask if whales have belly buttons or something and suddenly you’re off on that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say yes to that the way you asked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, making decisions, or staying focused on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’m still teaching, and I’m not dropping the big things — the kids are getting taught, the day still happens, you know. But I’ve noticed I’m not as on top of the planning pieces.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to revise a lesson plan and then end up checking three other things, or remembering I need to send something to a parent, and somehow the original thing doesn’t get finished. And then it’s the next day and someone on my team will say, “Did you upload that yet?” and I’ll realize I pushed it back again.\n\nThat’s not really like me. I used to be pretty reliable with those deadlines without needing reminders. Now I’m still meeting the basic responsibilities, but some planning deadlines and little task follow-through things have slipped.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re engaging in your usual activities less often, or that they feel less rewarding than they used to, even if you still enjoy them a little?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… less rewarding is hard for me to say. I still do the things I usually do, at least the main things. School takes up so much space that sometimes I feel like there isn’t much left to compare it to, you know?\n\nI mean, I’ll still talk with other teachers, still go through my routines. Sometimes I don’t feel as… I don’t know, sparked by things maybe, but then something small will happen and I’ll laugh. Like the other day one of the kids was very seriously explaining that our classroom plant “has trust issues,” which — I don’t even know where they get these things.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing less, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, like planning lessons or even following conversations, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Mostly with the lesson planning side of things. I’m still teaching, and the regular day-to-day with the kids is happening — you sort of have to stay present when twenty-something children all need something at once.\n\nBut the planning pieces, the follow-through… those have been harder. I’ll mean to finish something, like upload a plan or revise an activity, and then somehow it gets pushed back. And then someone on my team will remind me, and I’ll think, oh right, I used to just do that without being nudged.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been feeling down or having a low mood on most days, even if it's not severe, that lasts for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, but I do feel more discouraged and kind of wound up than I used to.\n\nIt really started after the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. I’m not totally sure. I still do my usual things, and I can still enjoy parts of them. Like, with the kids, there are still moments that are genuinely funny or sweet, and I don’t feel like that’s gone.

I guess “less satisfying” is tricky, because sometimes everything feels a little flattened when I’m thinking about school responsibilities — but then I wonder if that’s just being tired or distracted. My sister always says teachers live in a constant state of “almost done,” which is funny because it’s never actually done. But anyway… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change where my usual activities don’t feel enjoyable anymore. It’s more just hard to tell.

It’s more that since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been feeling wound up and discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me.\n\nI still get through the school day — I mean, teachers kind of have to, the bell rings whether you’re ready or not — but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without thinking. And then I look at the pile and feel worse about it. So, not hopeless all the time, but definitely more discouraged than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing more worry or anxiety than usual, especially about things related to work or responsibilities outside of school?\n\nPatient: Yes, more than usual, I think. It’s mostly tied to work, though not only during school hours, because I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something and suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer or a lesson plan I meant to revise.\n\nEver since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt wound up in a way that isn’t really like me. I mean, I’ve been through new programs before—there’s always some new initiative with binders and acronyms—but this one has me second-guessing things. I still get through the day, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails I used to handle without even thinking, and then I worry because I know I’m behind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired than usual even after resting or feeling like it takes more effort to get through the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change there. I mean, teaching is tiring anyway — especially with kids this time of year, they get that kind of squirrelly energy after lunch, and you feel like you’ve run a small circus by 3 o’clock.\n\nBut as far as being more tired even after resting… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking my energy like that. I still get through the day. Some evenings I feel worn out, but I sort of chalk that up to the usual school-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or less interested in things for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “Most of the day” and “more days than not” feels very… precise, and I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nI mean, some days I’m definitely discouraged, and I can get kind of stuck in that feeling, but then I’ll be fine talking with a student or dealing with something in the classroom. Kids don’t really let you stay in one mood for too long, you know? One of them will ask if whales have belly buttons or something and suddenly you’re off on that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say yes to that the way you asked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, making decisions, or staying focused on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’m still teaching, and I’m not dropping the big things — the kids are getting taught, the day still happens, you know. But I’ve noticed I’m not as on top of the planning pieces.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to revise a lesson plan and then end up checking three other things, or remembering I need to send something to a parent, and somehow the original thing doesn’t get finished. And then it’s the next day and someone on my team will say, “Did you upload that yet?” and I’ll realize I pushed it back again.\n\nThat’s not really like me. I used to be pretty reliable with those deadlines without needing reminders. Now I’m still meeting the basic responsibilities, but some planning deadlines and little task follow-through things have slipped.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re engaging in your usual activities less often, or that they feel less rewarding than they used to, even if you still enjoy them a little?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… less rewarding is hard for me to say. I still do the things I usually do, at least the main things. School takes up so much space that sometimes I feel like there isn’t much left to compare it to, you know?\n\nI mean, I’ll still talk with other teachers, still go through my routines. Sometimes I don’t feel as… I don’t know, sparked by things maybe, but then something small will happen and I’ll laugh. Like the other day one of the kids was very seriously explaining that our classroom plant “has trust issues,” which — I don’t even know where they get these things.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing less, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, like planning lessons or even following conversations, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Mostly with the lesson planning side of things. I’m still teaching, and the regular day-to-day with the kids is happening — you sort of have to stay present when twenty-something children all need something at once.\n\nBut the planning pieces, the follow-through… those have been harder. I’ll mean to finish something, like upload a plan or revise an activity, and then somehow it gets pushed back. And then someone on my team will remind me, and I’ll think, oh right, I used to just do that without being nudged.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been feeling down or having a low mood on most days, even if it's not severe, that lasts for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, but I do feel more discouraged and kind of wound up than I used to.\n\nIt really started after the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. And, you know, I care about that work — I do — but the way it landed was just… a lot. I still get through the school day, the kids are fine, the classroom is running. But there’s this low feeling that hangs around, especially when I think about everything I still have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll put off lesson plans or emails that I used to just handle without thinking. So yes, I’d say it’s been there a lot of days, even if I’m still showing up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re finding your usual activities less satisfying or enjoyable than they used to be, even if you still do them and get some pleasure out of them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I still do my usual things, and I can still enjoy parts of them. Like, with the kids, there are still moments that are genuinely funny or sweet, and I don’t feel like that’s gone.\n\nI guess “less satisfying” is tricky, because sometimes everything feels a little flattened when I’m thinking about school responsibilities — but then I wonder if that’s just being tired or distracted. My sister always says teachers live in a constant state of “almost done,” which is funny because it’s never actually done. But anyway… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change where my usual activities don’t feel enjoyable anymore. It’s more just hard to tell.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know that I’d call it lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I care about — my students, my classroom, my family, all of that.

Sometimes it feels like I’m not as… I don’t know, absorbed in things, maybe? But then I start thinking about whether that’s just because my mind keeps circling back to school tasks. Like yesterday I was trying to watch one of those cooking shows, and I kept remembering I hadn’t answered an email, and then somehow I was thinking about whether my students would like a recipe activity — which is not relaxing at all.

So, no, I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest or enjoyment. It’s more muddled than that.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F43.0, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because work has just started to feel… not like my usual work stress. I’m a teacher, and the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout onto what I’m already doing in the classroom. I mean, I care about the kids, and I’ve always handled new curriculum changes—there’s always something new in education, you know—but this one has me feeling really wound up and kind of discouraged.\n\nI’m still getting through the school day, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just take care of without even thinking. And then I get more behind, and that makes me feel worse. I don’t know, it just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest or not getting pleasure from activities that you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. I mean, I still like the things I like… though lately when I get home, sometimes I just end up thinking about school instead of settling into them. But I haven’t really noticed, like, a total loss of interest or anything.\n\nI used to do these little craft projects with my niece—well, she calls them “art commissions,” which is very dramatic for a ten-year-old—and I still enjoy that when I actually sit down to do it. So… no, I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure in things, at least not in a way I’ve really been tracking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t think I’d say depressed exactly. It’s more that since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been feeling wound up and discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me.\n\nI still get through the school day — I mean, teachers kind of have to, the bell rings whether you’re ready or not — but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without thinking. And then I look at the pile and feel worse about it. So, not hopeless all the time, but definitely more discouraged than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing more worry or anxiety than usual, especially about things related to work or responsibilities outside of school?\n\nPatient: Yes, more than usual, I think. It’s mostly tied to work, though not only during school hours, because I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something and suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer or a lesson plan I meant to revise.\n\nEver since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt wound up in a way that isn’t really like me. I mean, I’ve been through new programs before—there’s always some new initiative with binders and acronyms—but this one has me second-guessing things. I still get through the day, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails I used to handle without even thinking, and then I worry because I know I’m behind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired than usual even after resting or feeling like it takes more effort to get through the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change there. I mean, teaching is tiring anyway — especially with kids this time of year, they get that kind of squirrelly energy after lunch, and you feel like you’ve run a small circus by 3 o’clock.\n\nBut as far as being more tired even after resting… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking my energy like that. I still get through the day. Some evenings I feel worn out, but I sort of chalk that up to the usual school-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or less interested in things for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “Most of the day” and “more days than not” feels very… precise, and I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nI mean, some days I’m definitely discouraged, and I can get kind of stuck in that feeling, but then I’ll be fine talking with a student or dealing with something in the classroom. Kids don’t really let you stay in one mood for too long, you know? One of them will ask if whales have belly buttons or something and suddenly you’re off on that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say yes to that the way you asked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, making decisions, or staying focused on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’m still teaching, and I’m not dropping the big things — the kids are getting taught, the day still happens, you know. But I’ve noticed I’m not as on top of the planning pieces.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to revise a lesson plan and then end up checking three other things, or remembering I need to send something to a parent, and somehow the original thing doesn’t get finished. And then it’s the next day and someone on my team will say, “Did you upload that yet?” and I’ll realize I pushed it back again.\n\nThat’s not really like me. I used to be pretty reliable with those deadlines without needing reminders. Now I’m still meeting the basic responsibilities, but some planning deadlines and little task follow-through things have slipped.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re engaging in your usual activities less often, or that they feel less rewarding than they used to, even if you still enjoy them a little?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… less rewarding is hard for me to say. I still do the things I usually do, at least the main things. School takes up so much space that sometimes I feel like there isn’t much left to compare it to, you know?\n\nI mean, I’ll still talk with other teachers, still go through my routines. Sometimes I don’t feel as… I don’t know, sparked by things maybe, but then something small will happen and I’ll laugh. Like the other day one of the kids was very seriously explaining that our classroom plant “has trust issues,” which — I don’t even know where they get these things.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing less, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, like planning lessons or even following conversations, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Mostly with the lesson planning side of things. I’m still teaching, and the regular day-to-day with the kids is happening — you sort of have to stay present when twenty-something children all need something at once.\n\nBut the planning pieces, the follow-through… those have been harder. I’ll mean to finish something, like upload a plan or revise an activity, and then somehow it gets pushed back. And then someone on my team will remind me, and I’ll think, oh right, I used to just do that without being nudged.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been feeling down or having a low mood on most days, even if it's not severe, that lasts for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, but I do feel more discouraged and kind of wound up than I used to.\n\nIt really started after the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. And, you know, I care about that work — I do — but the way it landed was just… a lot. I still get through the school day, the kids are fine, the classroom is running. But there’s this low feeling that hangs around, especially when I think about everything I still have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll put off lesson plans or emails that I used to just handle without thinking. So yes, I’d say it’s been there a lot of days, even if I’m still showing up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re finding your usual activities less satisfying or enjoyable than they used to be, even if you still do them and get some pleasure out of them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I still do my usual things, and I can still enjoy parts of them. Like, with the kids, there are still moments that are genuinely funny or sweet, and I don’t feel like that’s gone.\n\nI guess “less satisfying” is tricky, because sometimes everything feels a little flattened when I’m thinking about school responsibilities — but then I wonder if that’s just being tired or distracted. My sister always says teachers live in a constant state of “almost done,” which is funny because it’s never actually done. But anyway… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change where my usual activities don’t feel enjoyable anymore. It’s more just hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost some of your interest in things you usually care about, even if you still do them, or that they feel noticeably less enjoyable than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I care about — my students, my classroom, my family, all of that.\n\nSometimes it feels like I’m not as… I don’t know, absorbed in things, maybe? But then I start thinking about whether that’s just because my mind keeps circling back to school tasks. Like yesterday I was trying to watch one of those cooking shows, and I kept remembering I hadn’t answered an email, and then somehow I was thinking about whether my students would like a recipe activity — which is not relaxing at all.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest or enjoyment. It’s more muddled than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more muddled than that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed that you’re not as interested in things you usually care about, even if you still do them, or that they feel less meaningful to you lately?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say things feel less meaningful. I still care about them. I mean, teaching still matters to me — probably too much sometimes — and my family matters, my routines matter.

It’s more that I get distracted from enjoying them, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be doing something normal, even something I like, and then my brain wanders off to school stuff. Actually, my nephew was telling me about this app he uses to organize his assignments, and then I started thinking maybe my students could use something like that, but then I remembered half of them don’t have reliable devices at home, and then I was off on that whole train of thought.

So… I don’t think I’ve noticed a real loss of interest or meaning. It’s just harder to stay present with things lately.

But I haven’t really noticed, like, a total loss of interest or anything.\n\nI used to do these little craft projects with my niece—well, she calls them “art commissions,” which is very dramatic for a ten-year-old—and I still enjoy that when I actually sit down to do it. So… no, I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure in things, at least not in a way I’ve really been tracking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t think I’d say depressed exactly. And then I look at the pile and feel worse about it. So, not hopeless all the time, but definitely more discouraged than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing more worry or anxiety than usual, especially about things related to work or responsibilities outside of school?\n\nPatient: Yes, more than usual, I think. It’s mostly tied to work, though not only during school hours, because I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something and suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer or a lesson plan I meant to revise.\n\nEver since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt wound up in a way that isn’t really like me. I mean, I’ve been through new programs before—there’s always some new initiative with binders and acronyms—but this one has me second-guessing things. I still get through the day, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails I used to handle without even thinking, and then I worry because I know I’m behind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired than usual even after resting or feeling like it takes more effort to get through the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change there. I mean, teaching is tiring anyway — especially with kids this time of year, they get that kind of squirrelly energy after lunch, and you feel like you’ve run a small circus by 3 o’clock.\n\nBut as far as being more tired even after resting… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking my energy like that. I still get through the day. Some evenings I feel worn out, but I sort of chalk that up to the usual school-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or less interested in things for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “Most of the day” and “more days than not” feels very… precise, and I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nI mean, some days I’m definitely discouraged, and I can get kind of stuck in that feeling, but then I’ll be fine talking with a student or dealing with something in the classroom. Kids don’t really let you stay in one mood for too long, you know? One of them will ask if whales have belly buttons or something and suddenly you’re off on that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say yes to that the way you asked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, making decisions, or staying focused on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. And then it’s the next day and someone on my team will say, “Did you upload that yet?” and I’ll realize I pushed it back again.\n\nThat’s not really like me. I used to be pretty reliable with those deadlines without needing reminders. Now I’m still meeting the basic responsibilities, but some planning deadlines and little task follow-through things have slipped.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re engaging in your usual activities less often, or that they feel less rewarding than they used to, even if you still enjoy them a little?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… less rewarding is hard for me to say. I still do the things I usually do, at least the main things. School takes up so much space that sometimes I feel like there isn’t much left to compare it to, you know?\n\nI mean, I’ll still talk with other teachers, still go through my routines. Sometimes I don’t feel as… I don’t know, sparked by things maybe, but then something small will happen and I’ll laugh. Like yesterday I was trying to watch one of those cooking shows, and I kept remembering I hadn’t answered an email, and then somehow I was thinking about whether my students would like a recipe activity — which is not relaxing at all.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest or enjoyment. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not as interested in things you usually care about, even if you still do them, or that they feel less meaningful to you lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say things feel less meaningful. I still care about them. I mean, teaching still matters to me — probably too much sometimes — and my family matters, my routines matter.\n\nIt’s more that I get distracted from enjoying them, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be doing something normal, even something I like, and then my brain wanders off to school stuff. Actually, my nephew was telling me about this app he uses to organize his assignments, and then I started thinking maybe my students could use something like that, but then I remembered half of them don’t have reliable devices at home, and then I was off on that whole train of thought.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve noticed a real loss of interest or meaning. It’s just harder to stay present with things lately.\n\n[Last Question]\nLooking back over the past few weeks, have you noticed that you’re not really getting the same sense of enjoyment or satisfaction from things you used to look forward to, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Even when I’m not thinking about work… I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’d say, “Oh, I don’t enjoy that anymore.” Like if I’m with my family or watching something I like, I can still have a nice time.

I did go to a little school concert recently—not my school, actually, a friend’s grandchild was in it—and I remember thinking how sweet it was that half the kids were singing at totally different speeds. That still made me laugh. So I guess I do still get enjoyment from things.

Maybe it’s not as clear-cut as feeling fully relaxed or satisfied, but I haven’t noticed a definite change like that.

I mean, I’ve been through new programs before—there’s always some new initiative with binders and acronyms—but this one has me second-guessing things. I still get through the day, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails I used to handle without even thinking, and then I worry because I know I’m behind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired than usual even after resting or feeling like it takes more effort to get through the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change there. I mean, teaching is tiring anyway — especially with kids this time of year, they get that kind of squirrelly energy after lunch, and you feel like you’ve run a small circus by 3 o’clock.\n\nBut as far as being more tired even after resting… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking my energy like that. I still get through the day. Some evenings I feel worn out, but I sort of chalk that up to the usual school-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or less interested in things for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “Most of the day” and “more days than not” feels very… precise, and I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nI mean, some days I’m definitely discouraged, and I can get kind of stuck in that feeling, but then I’ll be fine talking with a student or dealing with something in the classroom. Kids don’t really let you stay in one mood for too long, you know? One of them will ask if whales have belly buttons or something and suddenly you’re off on that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. And then it’s the next day and someone on my team will say, “Did you upload that yet?” and I’ll realize I pushed it back again.\n\nThat’s not really like me. I used to be pretty reliable with those deadlines without needing reminders. Now I’m still meeting the basic responsibilities, but some planning deadlines and little task follow-through things have slipped.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re engaging in your usual activities less often, or that they feel less rewarding than they used to, even if you still enjoy them a little?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… less rewarding is hard for me to say. I still do the things I usually do, at least the main things. School takes up so much space that sometimes I feel like there isn’t much left to compare it to, you know?\n\nI mean, I’ll still talk with other teachers, still go through my routines. Sometimes I don’t feel as… I don’t know, sparked by things maybe, but then something small will happen and I’ll laugh. Like the other day one of the kids was very seriously explaining that our classroom plant “has trust issues,” which — I don’t even know where they get these things.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing less, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, like planning lessons or even following conversations, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I’ll mean to finish something, like upload a plan or revise an activity, and then somehow it gets pushed back. Maybe a little in the sense that starting anything can feel like one more thing on the list, but I don’t know that it feels less meaningful. I still care about the same things.

I mean, I care about the kids, and I’ve always handled new curriculum changes—there’s always something new in education, you know—but this one has me feeling really wound up and kind of discouraged.\n\nI’m still getting through the school day, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just take care of without even thinking. So… no, I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure in things, at least not in a way I’ve really been tracking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t think I’d say depressed exactly. I still get through the day, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails I used to handle without even thinking, and then I worry because I know I’m behind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired than usual even after resting or feeling like it takes more effort to get through the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change there. I haven’t really been tracking my energy like that. I still get through the day. Some evenings I feel worn out, but I sort of chalk that up to the usual school-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or less interested in things for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “Most of the day” and “more days than not” feels very… precise, and I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nI mean, some days I’m definitely discouraged, and I can get kind of stuck in that feeling, but then I’ll be fine talking with a student or dealing with something in the classroom. Kids don’t really let you stay in one mood for too long, you know? One of them will ask if whales have belly buttons or something and suddenly you’re off on that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say yes to that the way you asked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, making decisions, or staying focused on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’m still teaching, and I’m not dropping the big things — the kids are getting taught, the day still happens, you know. But I’ve noticed I’m not as on top of the planning pieces.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to revise a lesson plan and then end up checking three other things, or remembering I need to send something to a parent, and somehow the original thing doesn’t get finished. And then it’s the next day and someone on my team will say, “Did you upload that yet?” and I’ll realize I pushed it back again.\n\nThat’s not really like me. I used to be pretty reliable with those deadlines without needing reminders. Now I’m still meeting the basic responsibilities, but some planning deadlines and little task follow-through things have slipped.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re engaging in your usual activities less often, or that they feel less rewarding than they used to, even if you still enjoy them a little?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… less rewarding is hard for me to say. I still do the things I usually do, at least the main things. School takes up so much space that sometimes I feel like there isn’t much left to compare it to, you know?\n\nI mean, I’ll still talk with other teachers, still go through my routines. Sometimes I don’t feel as… I don’t know, sparked by things maybe, but then something small will happen and I’ll laugh. Like the other day one of the kids was very seriously explaining that our classroom plant “has trust issues,” which — I don’t even know where they get these things.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing less, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, like planning lessons or even following conversations, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Mostly with the lesson planning side of things. I’m still teaching, and the regular day-to-day with the kids is happening — you sort of have to stay present when twenty-something children all need something at once.\n\nBut the planning pieces, the follow-through… those have been harder. I’ll mean to finish something, like upload a plan or revise an activity, and then somehow it gets pushed back. And then someone on my team will remind me, and I’ll think, oh right, I used to just do that without being nudged.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been feeling down or having a low mood on most days, even if it's not severe, that lasts for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, but I do feel more discouraged and kind of wound up than I used to.\n\nIt really started after the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. And, you know, I care about that work — I do — but the way it landed was just… a lot. I still get through the school day, the kids are fine, the classroom is running. But there’s this low feeling that hangs around, especially when I think about everything I still have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll put off lesson plans or emails that I used to just handle without thinking. So yes, I’d say it’s been there a lot of days, even if I’m still showing up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re finding your usual activities less satisfying or enjoyable than they used to be, even if you still do them and get some pleasure out of them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I still do my usual things, and I can still enjoy parts of them. Like, with the kids, there are still moments that are genuinely funny or sweet, and I don’t feel like that’s gone.\n\nI guess “less satisfying” is tricky, because sometimes everything feels a little flattened when I’m thinking about school responsibilities — but then I wonder if that’s just being tired or distracted. My sister always says teachers live in a constant state of “almost done,” which is funny because it’s never actually done. But anyway… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change where my usual activities don’t feel enjoyable anymore. It’s more just hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost some of your interest in things you usually care about, even if you still do them, or that they feel noticeably less enjoyable than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I care about — my students, my classroom, my family, all of that.\n\nSometimes it feels like I’m not as… I don’t know, absorbed in things, maybe? I don’t think so, not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, there are times I’m sort of… not fully settled into enjoying something, but I wouldn’t say it feels less pleasurable exactly.

Like yesterday I was trying to watch one of those baking shows — the ones where they make these impossible cakes, and honestly I don’t know why anyone needs a cake shaped like a handbag — and I was enjoying it, but then I started thinking about a project one of my students made with cardboard and tape, and then I wondered if I should save more boxes for the classroom. But when I’m actually there, when I can stay with it, I still enjoy things. So no, I don’t think I’ve had days where things feel less interesting or less pleasurable. More like my mind wanders.

Some evenings I feel worn out, but I sort of chalk that up to the usual school-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or less interested in things for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “Most of the day” and “more days than not” feels very… precise, and I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nI mean, some days I’m definitely discouraged, and I can get kind of stuck in that feeling, but then I’ll be fine talking with a student or dealing with something in the classroom. Kids don’t really let you stay in one mood for too long, you know? One of them will ask if whales have belly buttons or something and suddenly you’re off on that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say yes to that the way you asked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, making decisions, or staying focused on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’m still teaching, and I’m not dropping the big things — the kids are getting taught, the day still happens, you know. But I’ve noticed I’m not as on top of the planning pieces.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to revise a lesson plan and then end up checking three other things, or remembering I need to send something to a parent, and somehow the original thing doesn’t get finished. And then it’s the next day and someone on my team will say, “Did you upload that yet?” and I’ll realize I pushed it back again.\n\nThat’s not really like me. I used to be pretty reliable with those deadlines without needing reminders. Now I’m still meeting the basic responsibilities, but some planning deadlines and little task follow-through things have slipped.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re engaging in your usual activities less often, or that they feel less rewarding than they used to, even if you still enjoy them a little?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… less rewarding is hard for me to say. I still do the things I usually do, at least the main things. School takes up so much space that sometimes I feel like there isn’t much left to compare it to, you know?\n\nI mean, I’ll still talk with other teachers, still go through my routines. Sometimes I don’t feel as… I don’t know, sparked by things maybe, but then something small will happen and I’ll laugh. Like the other day one of the kids was very seriously explaining that our classroom plant “has trust issues,” which — I don’t even know where they get these things.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing less, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, like planning lessons or even following conversations, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Mostly with the lesson planning side of things. I’m still teaching, and the regular day-to-day with the kids is happening — you sort of have to stay present when twenty-something children all need something at once.\n\nBut the planning pieces, the follow-through… those have been harder. I’ll mean to finish something, like upload a plan or revise an activity, and then somehow it gets pushed back. And then someone on my team will remind me, and I’ll think, oh right, I used to just do that without being nudged.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been feeling down or having a low mood on most days, even if it's not severe, that lasts for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, but I do feel more discouraged and kind of wound up than I used to.\n\nIt really started after the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. And, you know, I care about that work — I do — but the way it landed was just… a lot. I still get through the school day, the kids are fine, the classroom is running. But there’s this low feeling that hangs around, especially when I think about everything I still have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll put off lesson plans or emails that I used to just handle without thinking. So yes, I’d say it’s been there a lot of days, even if I’m still showing up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re finding your usual activities less satisfying or enjoyable than they used to be, even if you still do them and get some pleasure out of them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I still do my usual things, and I can still enjoy parts of them. Like, with the kids, there are still moments that are genuinely funny or sweet, and I don’t feel like that’s gone.\n\nI guess “less satisfying” is tricky, because sometimes everything feels a little flattened when I’m thinking about school responsibilities — but then I wonder if that’s just being tired or distracted. My sister always says teachers live in a constant state of “almost done,” which is funny because it’s never actually done. But anyway… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change where my usual activities don’t feel enjoyable anymore. It’s more just hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost some of your interest in things you usually care about, even if you still do them, or that they feel noticeably less enjoyable than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I care about — my students, my classroom, my family, all of that.\n\nSometimes it feels like I’m not as… I don’t know, absorbed in things, maybe? But then I start thinking about whether that’s just because my mind keeps circling back to school tasks. Like yesterday I was trying to watch one of those cooking shows, and I kept remembering I hadn’t answered an email, and then somehow I was thinking about whether my students would like a recipe activity — which is not relaxing at all.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest or enjoyment. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not as interested in things you usually care about, even if you still do them, or that they feel less meaningful to you lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say things feel less meaningful. I still care about them. I mean, teaching still matters to me — probably too much sometimes — and my family matters, my routines matter.\n\nIt’s more that I get distracted from enjoying them, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be doing something normal, even something I like, and then my brain wanders off to school stuff. Actually, my nephew was telling me about this app he uses to organize his assignments, and then I started thinking maybe my students could use something like that, but then I remembered half of them don’t have reliable devices at home, and then I was off on that whole train of thought.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve noticed a real loss of interest or meaning. It’s just harder to stay present with things lately.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few weeks, have you noticed that you’re not really getting the same sense of enjoyment or satisfaction from things you used to look forward to, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Even when I’m not thinking about work… I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’d say, “Oh, I don’t enjoy that anymore.” Like if I’m with my family or watching something I like, I can still have a nice time.\n\nI did go to a little school concert recently—not my school, actually, a friend’s grandchild was in it—and I remember thinking how sweet it was that half the kids were singing at totally different speeds. That still made me laugh. So I guess I do still get enjoyment from things.\n\nMaybe it’s not as clear-cut as feeling fully relaxed or satisfied, but I haven’t noticed a definite change like that.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say there have been days when even the things you usually care about feel like more of an effort to engage in, or just don’t feel as meaningful as they used to, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little in the sense that starting anything can feel like one more thing on the list, but I don’t know that it feels less meaningful. I still care about the same things.\n\nIt’s funny, I was looking at this stack of library books I checked out — I always do that, I check out too many and then renew them like I’m negotiating with the library — and I thought, “Oh, I should read,” but then I started thinking about whether my classroom library needs more graphic novels, because the kids really do read those, and then I was mentally reorganizing shelves.\n\nSo… effort sometimes, maybe, but not because I don’t care. More like my attention keeps getting pulled away. I wouldn’t say things have stopped mattering to me.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, have you had days where even the things you usually enjoy feel less interesting or less pleasurable, even if just a little bit or for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, there are times I’m sort of… not fully settled into enjoying something, but I wouldn’t say it feels less pleasurable exactly.\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to watch one of those baking shows — the ones where they make these impossible cakes, and honestly I don’t know why anyone needs a cake shaped like a handbag — and I was enjoying it, but then I started thinking about a project one of my students made with cardboard and tape, and then I wondered if I should save more boxes for the classroom. So I drifted.\n\nBut when I’m actually there, when I can stay with it, I still enjoy things. So no, I don’t think I’ve had days where things feel less interesting or less pleasurable. More like my mind wanders.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

[

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Adjustment Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few weeks, have you noticed that you’re not really getting the same sense of enjoyment or satisfaction from things you used to look forward to, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Even when I’m not thinking about work… I’m not sure. I don’t think there’s anything where I’d say, “Oh, I don’t enjoy that anymore.” Like if I’m with my family or watching something I like, I can still have a nice time.\n\nI did go to a little school concert recently—not my school, actually, a friend’s grandchild was in it—and I remember thinking how sweet it was that half the kids were singing at totally different speeds. That still made me laugh. So I guess I do still get enjoyment from things.\n\nMaybe it’s not as clear-cut as feeling fully relaxed or satisfied, but I haven’t noticed a definite change like that.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, would you say there have been days when even the things you usually care about feel like more of an effort to engage in, or just don’t feel as meaningful as they used to, even for a little while?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little in the sense that starting anything can feel like one more thing on the list, but I don’t know that it feels less meaningful. I still care about the same things.\n\nIt’s funny, I was looking at this stack of library books I checked out — I always do that, I check out too many and then renew them like I’m negotiating with the library — and I thought, “Oh, I should read,” but then I started thinking about whether my classroom library needs more graphic novels, because the kids really do read those, and then I was mentally reorganizing shelves.\n\nSo… effort sometimes, maybe, but not because I don’t care. More like my attention keeps getting pulled away. I wouldn’t say things have stopped mattering to me.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, have you had days where even the things you usually enjoy feel less interesting or less pleasurable, even if just a little bit or for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a way I’ve really noticed. I mean, there are times I’m sort of… not fully settled into enjoying something, but I wouldn’t say it feels less pleasurable exactly.\n\nLike yesterday I was trying to watch one of those baking shows — the ones where they make these impossible cakes, and honestly I don’t know why anyone needs a cake shaped like a handbag — and I was enjoying it, but then I started thinking about a project one of my students made with cardboard and tape, and then I wondered if I should save more boxes for the classroom. More like my mind wanders.\n\n[Last Question]\nLooking back, have you had times when activities you used to really enjoy didn’t feel as good or as satisfying, even when you were able to do them?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I can do things and still feel glad I did them. It’s more that sometimes I’m not as settled, if that makes sense.

Like I went out for coffee with a friend, and I did enjoy it, though she was telling me this whole long story about her neighbor’s dog getting into someone’s recycling, and then I started thinking about whether I’d remembered to rinse out my yogurt containers at home—which is ridiculous, because who cares in that moment? But the coffee was nice. The company was nice.

So I wouldn’t say the activities don’t feel good or satisfying. I think I still get that feeling. My mind just wanders off sometimes.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.22, F43.0, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I’m here because work has just started to feel… not like my usual work stress. I’m a teacher, and the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout onto what I’m already doing in the classroom. I mean, I care about the kids, and I’ve always handled new curriculum changes—there’s always something new in education, you know—but this one has me feeling really wound up and kind of discouraged.\n\nI’m still getting through the school day, so it’s not like I’ve stopped functioning, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just take care of without even thinking. And then I get more behind, and that makes me feel worse. I don’t know, it just doesn’t feel like me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest or not getting pleasure from activities that you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way exactly. I mean, I still like the things I like… though lately when I get home, sometimes I just end up thinking about school instead of settling into them. But I haven’t really noticed, like, a total loss of interest or anything.\n\nI used to do these little craft projects with my niece—well, she calls them “art commissions,” which is very dramatic for a ten-year-old—and I still enjoy that when I actually sit down to do it. So… no, I don’t think I’ve lost pleasure in things, at least not in a way I’ve really been tracking.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down, depressed, or hopeless most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Most of the time… I don’t think I’d say depressed exactly. It’s more that since the district added this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on top of my regular teaching, I’ve been feeling wound up and discouraged in a way that doesn’t really feel like me.\n\nI still get through the school day — I mean, teachers kind of have to, the bell rings whether you’re ready or not — but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails that I used to just handle without thinking. And then I look at the pile and feel worse about it. So, not hopeless all the time, but definitely more discouraged than usual.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing more worry or anxiety than usual, especially about things related to work or responsibilities outside of school?\n\nPatient: Yes, more than usual, I think. It’s mostly tied to work, though not only during school hours, because I’ll be making dinner or trying to watch something and suddenly remember an email I didn’t answer or a lesson plan I meant to revise.\n\nEver since the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching, I’ve felt wound up in a way that isn’t really like me. I mean, I’ve been through new programs before—there’s always some new initiative with binders and acronyms—but this one has me second-guessing things. I still get through the day, but I’ve started putting off lesson plans and emails I used to handle without even thinking, and then I worry because I know I’m behind.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy levels lately, like feeling more tired than usual even after resting or feeling like it takes more effort to get through the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve noticed a clear change there. I mean, teaching is tiring anyway — especially with kids this time of year, they get that kind of squirrelly energy after lunch, and you feel like you’ve run a small circus by 3 o’clock.\n\nBut as far as being more tired even after resting… I’m not sure. I haven’t really been tracking my energy like that. I still get through the day. Some evenings I feel worn out, but I sort of chalk that up to the usual school-day stuff.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or less interested in things for most of the day, more days than not, for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that way. “Most of the day” and “more days than not” feels very… precise, and I haven’t really been keeping track like that.\n\nI mean, some days I’m definitely discouraged, and I can get kind of stuck in that feeling, but then I’ll be fine talking with a student or dealing with something in the classroom. Kids don’t really let you stay in one mood for too long, you know? One of them will ask if whales have belly buttons or something and suddenly you’re off on that.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I can’t really say yes to that the way you asked it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any trouble thinking clearly, making decisions, or staying focused on tasks lately?\n\nPatient: A little, yes. I’m still teaching, and I’m not dropping the big things — the kids are getting taught, the day still happens, you know. But I’ve noticed I’m not as on top of the planning pieces.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to revise a lesson plan and then end up checking three other things, or remembering I need to send something to a parent, and somehow the original thing doesn’t get finished. And then it’s the next day and someone on my team will say, “Did you upload that yet?” and I’ll realize I pushed it back again.\n\nThat’s not really like me. I used to be pretty reliable with those deadlines without needing reminders. Now I’m still meeting the basic responsibilities, but some planning deadlines and little task follow-through things have slipped.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re engaging in your usual activities less often, or that they feel less rewarding than they used to, even if you still enjoy them a little?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… less rewarding is hard for me to say. I still do the things I usually do, at least the main things. School takes up so much space that sometimes I feel like there isn’t much left to compare it to, you know?\n\nI mean, I’ll still talk with other teachers, still go through my routines. Sometimes I don’t feel as… I don’t know, sparked by things maybe, but then something small will happen and I’ll laugh. Like the other day one of the kids was very seriously explaining that our classroom plant “has trust issues,” which — I don’t even know where they get these things.\n\nSo I guess I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing less, exactly. I haven’t really noticed a clear change like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you found it harder to concentrate on tasks, like planning lessons or even following conversations, more than you used to?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. Mostly with the lesson planning side of things. I’m still teaching, and the regular day-to-day with the kids is happening — you sort of have to stay present when twenty-something children all need something at once.\n\nBut the planning pieces, the follow-through… those have been harder. I’ll mean to finish something, like upload a plan or revise an activity, and then somehow it gets pushed back. And then someone on my team will remind me, and I’ll think, oh right, I used to just do that without being nudged.\n\nIt’s not like everything is falling apart, but it’s more than I’m used to.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've been feeling down or having a low mood on most days, even if it's not severe, that lasts for much of the day?\n\nPatient: Most days, yes… not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, but I do feel more discouraged and kind of wound up than I used to.\n\nIt really started after the district put this bilingual social-emotional learning rollout on my plate, on top of my regular teaching. And, you know, I care about that work — I do — but the way it landed was just… a lot. I still get through the school day, the kids are fine, the classroom is running. But there’s this low feeling that hangs around, especially when I think about everything I still have to do.\n\nAnd then I’ll put off lesson plans or emails that I used to just handle without thinking. So yes, I’d say it’s been there a lot of days, even if I’m still showing up.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’re finding your usual activities less satisfying or enjoyable than they used to be, even if you still do them and get some pleasure out of them?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I still do my usual things, and I can still enjoy parts of them. Like, with the kids, there are still moments that are genuinely funny or sweet, and I don’t feel like that’s gone.\n\nI guess “less satisfying” is tricky, because sometimes everything feels a little flattened when I’m thinking about school responsibilities — but then I wonder if that’s just being tired or distracted. My sister always says teachers live in a constant state of “almost done,” which is funny because it’s never actually done. But anyway… no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change where my usual activities don’t feel enjoyable anymore. It’s more just hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you’ve lost some of your interest in things you usually care about, even if you still do them, or that they feel noticeably less enjoyable than they used to?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it lost interest, exactly. I still care about the things I care about — my students, my classroom, my family, all of that.\n\nSometimes it feels like I’m not as… I don’t know, absorbed in things, maybe? But then I start thinking about whether that’s just because my mind keeps circling back to school tasks. Like yesterday I was trying to watch one of those cooking shows, and I kept remembering I hadn’t answered an email, and then somehow I was thinking about whether my students would like a recipe activity — which is not relaxing at all.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t really noticed a clear loss of interest or enjoyment. It’s more muddled than that.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’re not as interested in things you usually care about, even if you still do them, or that they feel less meaningful to you lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say things feel less meaningful. I still care about them. I mean, teaching still matters to me — probably too much sometimes — and my family matters, my routines matter.\n\nIt’s more that I get distracted from enjoying them, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be doing something normal, even something I like, and then my brain wanders off to school stuff. Actually, my nephew was telling me about this app he uses to organize his assignments, and then I started thinking maybe my students could use something like that, but then I remembered half of them don’t have reliable devices at home, and then I was off on that whole train of thought.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve noticed a real loss of interest or meaning. It’s just harder to stay present with things lately.\n\nDoctor: Looking back over the past few weeks, have you noticed that you’re not really getting the same sense of enjoyment or satisfaction from things you used to look forward to, even when you’re not thinking about work?\n\nPatient: Even when I’m not thinking about work… I’m not sure. More like my attention keeps getting pulled away.
